#214. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time never gets old

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The franchise has evolved since 1998, but the first 3D Zelda stands up incredibly well in its Switch 2 remake.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Breath of the Wild changed everything. Two decades after The Legend of Zelda made the transformational shift to 3D, Breath of the Wild reinvigorated the franchise with an open-ended adventure that was built around player freedom and expression. Instead of following a set path, players could go wherever they wanted and tackle challenges in a variety of ways. That philosophy filtered down to subsequent games, letting players author their own creative solutions to puzzles in titles like Tears of the Kingdom and Echoes of Wisdom. As a result, Zelda has never been bigger, and the traditional, more linear Zelda games had become a thing of the past. Or so it seemed.

For one of the biggest Switch 2 exclusives so far, Nintendo has gone back to Ocarina of Time, which originally launched on the N64 in 1998. It’s one of the most influential and revered games in the long-running franchise, a game that showed that the classic Zelda formula — one that used a large world to connect a handful of intricate, puzzle-filled dungeons, creating a grand adventure — could work in three dimensions. Now Ocarina has been remade for the Switch 2 in a way that accomplishes exactly what a good remake is designed to do: preserve the core of the original experience while making it more accessible for modern audiences. And it shows there’s still some life left in the traditional Zelda formula.

The story remains unchanged from the original. It’s a third-person action-adventure game where you play as a little forest dweller named Link who gets pulled into an adventure to save the fantasy realm of Hyrule from a villain named Ganondorf. That’s true of basically every Zelda title, and here the twist comes from a musical instrument (the titular flute-like ocarina) imbued with magical powers. Over the course of the game you learn songs that can change the weather, warp you across the landscape, mess with the flow of time, and more.

The original Ocarina was renowned both for its large 3D world and for the intricacy of its dungeons, which required using the ocarina, along with various other items, to solve puzzles in clever ways. At a certain point you’re even jumping back and forth in time, navigating spaces as both tiny Link and his adult version. The challenge steadily grows in complexity right up until you face Ganondorf in his castle, forcing you to use a huge range of items at your disposal.

The most notable difference in the remake is how it looks. Gone are the blocky, fuzzy N64-era graphics, which have been replaced with something that blurs the line between fantasy art and realism. While it’s not a fully open world like Breath of the Wild, this version of Hyrule, which has a large open field that connects a series of dungeons and villages, is still incredibly lifelike; everything from rushing water in a lake to the swaying grass outside of a ranch looks and moves in realistic ways. The flip side is that, while the characters are more detailed than before, they’ve been rendered with a style inspired by puppets and stage shows. It’s a tricky balancing act that the game mostly nails. The characters look like exaggerated caricatures, but at the same time they’re imbued with such a level of detail that they don’t feel out of place against the more realistic backdrops. Link is the best example; while his face almost looks like a porcelain figure, his outfit is like something a real adventurer would wear, made by weaving together grasses found in Kokiri Forest, where he lives.

It looks great in practice — although Ocarina does suffer from some frustratingly dark areas, like in most modern games — and it also sets the remake apart from the rest of modern Zelda adventures. The Breath of the Wild line has a fantastical, Studio Ghibli-inspired look, while the top-down spinoffs have cutesy, toylike graphics. Ocarina splits the difference and comes out looking like something distinct, yet unmistakably Zelda. The Switch 2 graphics also do a great job of heightening the emotion. There’s an incredible sense of grandeur when you first walk into the stark Temple of Time, with a single ray of light illuminating the dark interior. Iconic moments like wandering blindly through the desert take on a new sense of tension and terror because of how the game looks as sand whips around you, obscuring your view of the harsh landscape. Fighting Dark Link on a foggy, desolate beach was enough to get my heart racing.

Perhaps more important than the visual update is a number of smart gameplay changes. Early 3D games can be awkward to play now because designers were still working out how best to navigate 3D spaces. The Ocarina remake fixes this in a few ways. For one thing, you now have free camera control, making it possible to look anywhere by moving the right stick, just like in any modern game. This makes it easier to keep your eye on enemies around you or spot important details for solving a puzzle. The game now also lets you jump whenever you want — in the original Link could only jump at designated points — which makes moving around in spaces feel less restrictive. Other modern touches include being able to equip items quickly with the D-pad instead of having to go to the pause menu, and the “threads of time,” which is a fancy name for a tool that tracks your quests so you can more easily figure out what to do next.

As an example of how much these tweaks can impact the experience, let’s revisit the infamous Water Temple. Over the years since Ocarina of Time first launched, this dungeon has become known primarily for being extremely difficult. It involves constantly playing a song to make the water rise and fall so you can access different levels, while also frequently equipping iron boots that cause Link to sink to the bottom when necessary. With the original controls this was tedious and frustrating. But I didn’t find it difficult at all in the remake; I could quickly equip the boots whenever I needed to, and the camera allowed me to clearly see what I needed to do. The Water Temple was always a well-designed dungeon hindered by the technology of the time. In the remake, I could fully appreciate what the developers were going for.

There are a handful of other changes — expanded dialogue, the ability to hum songs into the Switch 2’s microphone, the fairy Navi thankfully toning down her annoying hints — and they all build toward the same goal: enhancing the original without changing its essence. Unlike a lot of classic games, the original Ocarina of Time is still relatively easy to play; it’s available through a Switch Online subscription, and the 3DS version from 2011 remains a great port. But the remake does enough to make it the ideal way to play, particularly for that big new audience of Zelda fans that started with Breath of the Wild. More importantly, it’s proof that Zelda can be many things simultaneously. And that’s an exciting prospect for Hyrule’s future.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time launches on the Switch 2 on November 5th.

#213. Ted Schlein’s Ballistic Ventures seeks $490M for new funds amid AI-driven cybersecurity boom

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Ballistic Ventures, the cybersecurity-focused venture capital firm co-founded by former Kleiner Perkins partner Ted Schlein (pictured above, second from the right), is seeking to raise $490 million across two new funds, TechCrunch has learned through regulatory filings.

The San Francisco-based VC firm filed with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission this week to raise $360 million for its third flagship fund, matching the size of its previous fund raised in 2024, and another $130 million for a separate vehicle called Ballistic Ventures Fund B. Neither fund had recorded any sales at the time of filing.

The fundraising plans come as the growing use of artificial intelligence has raised concerns about more sophisticated cyberattacks. The rise of AI agents has also created new opportunities for cybersecurity startups and the venture capital firms backing them.

Founded in late 2021, Ballistic Ventures’ founding team includes Schlein, former AT&T Cybersecurity president Barmak Meftah, and Roger Thornton, who co-founded security companies Fortify Software and AlienVault. The firm has backed companies, including Talon Cyber Security, acquired by Palo Alto Networks, and Pangea, bought by CrowdStrike, alongside startups such as Armadin, GetReal, Nudge Security, and Oligo Security.

Ballistic’s ties to storied venture capital firm Kleiner Perkins, where Schlein spent more than two decades, help set it apart from rivals, a cybersecurity investor at another VC firm told TechCrunch. Its investment strategy, however, is similar to those of other cybersecurity-focused investors, including Forgepoint Capital and Ten Eleven Ventures, the person said.

In 2022, Ballistic announced its $300 million debut fund and raised another $360 million for its second flagship fund two years later. It also raised about $130 million for a separate vehicle called Ballistic Ventures Fund A, per PitchBook data. The new Fund B is targeting a similar amount.

U.S. cybersecurity startups raised $11.5 billion in 2025, the highest since 2022, a recent report by JPMorgan Chase said, citing PitchBook data. AI-enabled companies accounted for about 72% of U.S. cybersecurity venture deals through May this year, compared with 36% in 2019, the report said.

Separately, Microsoft, in its annual Digital Defense Report released last month, warned that hackers are increasingly using AI for activities ranging from phishing and social engineering to malware development. The technology is allowing attackers to operate faster and at greater scale, even as many of their underlying techniques remain unchanged.

Ballistic Ventures declined to comment on its fundraising plans.

#212. Data centers are turning to old jet engines as the wait for power grows

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Stuck in years-long lines for grid connections and new turbines, some AI data centers are making their own electricity with converted jet engines, the New York Times reported Tuesday.

The engines, pulled from retired aircraft and rebuilt to burn natural gas, aren’t anyone’s idea of a permanent power plant. But they’re another sign of how far power supply has fallen behind the AI buildout.

For companies stuck in those lines, the main draw of converted jet engines is how quickly the engines can be up and running. “It’s all about getting power as fast as possible,” Rystad Energy analyst Audun Martinsen told the Times. With jet-powered units, “they can have a data center running in 24 months,” he said.

Hooking up 100 megawatts to the grid in Northern Virginia, by comparison, takes about seven years, according to the International Energy Agency. Most power capacity is expected to come on by 2031, an executive at Mitsubishi Power, one of the world’s top makers of power generation equipment, told Bloomberg last week.

In some cases, these delays are already costing some data center operators business. Last month, Microsoft pulled out as the anchor customer for Nscale’s data center in Loughton, England, after the local utility said it couldn’t deliver enough power until the early to mid-2030s.

Elon Musk’s xAI is among the companies already using the engines, having installed eight converted jet-engine turbines in Mississippi to help power its Colossus 2 data center in Memphis, the Times reported, citing state permits and satellite imagery. The units come from ProEnergy, which rebuilds a GE engine commonly found on Boeing jets.

In 2025, OpenAI followed Musk’s lead with gas turbines at its first Stargate site in Abilene, Texas. Crusoe, a Denver-based startup that builds AI data centers, is constructing that Abilene campus and ordered 13 of ProEnergy’s converted units in April.

In September, Crusoe walked away from a $1.25 billion deal to buy 29 natural gas turbines from Boom Supersonic, a startup developing a supersonic passenger jet that has also begun selling a version of that jet’s engine as a power plant for data centers. A Crusoe spokesperson told TechCrunch at the time that the company still plans to use turbines.

Currently the Trump administration is adding to the pressure by encouraging data centers to generate their own electricity.

Converted jet engines come with trade-offs, though: They’re less efficient and pollute more than turbines designed for conventional gas plants, and it takes more of them to make the same amount of power, according to the Times. That may ultimately make converted jet engines a short-term play with some experts saying that efficiency and environmental concerns may grow in importance in a year or two.

#211. Google hasn’t figured out how to make AI pay like its search engine

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Google says AI is boosting searches, but that activity isn’t translating into faster growth for the business that pays for it.

Google says AI is boosting searches, but that activity isn’t translating into faster growth for the business that pays for it.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Search queries are growing at their fastest rate in five years, Google parent Alphabet said in its earnings report last week. However, Google’s Search revenue growth slowed to 12 percent year over year in the third quarter, down from roughly 17 percent in the previous quarter. Meanwhile, growth in paid clicks — clicks on ads that generate revenue for Google — slowed from 13 percent to 8 percent.

That’s still substantial growth, but not the direction investors were hoping for from a company planning to spend about $200 billion this year on infrastructure, largely to support AI. Alphabet shares have fallen about 14 percent since it reported earnings last week, as investors question the costs and returns of the company’s AI ambitions.

“This is the first major disruption that Google is facing to its core business in decades.”

Chief Business Officer Philipp Schindler said currency effects were the biggest driver of the revenue-growth slowdown, alongside changes in spending by advertisers in particular industries. But Google’s AI makeover also contributed to the slowdown: AI Mode queries were growing strongly but monetizing at a lower rate than traditional searches, Schindler says. He added that Google feels good about its trajectory and that its “ad experiments have shown promising results.”

It remains unclear whether that reflects a deliberate rollout of ads, as Google says, or a more fundamental problem with advertising in AI search.

“This is the first major disruption that Google is facing to its core business in decades,” Nate Elliott, an eMarketer principal analyst covering AI and advertising, tells The Verge. AI is changing how people find information — and whether they click on the ads that fund Google’s business.

What Google’s core Search business looks like increasingly depends on what you ask. In many cases, AI Overviews add generated summaries to search results; electing AI Mode turns searching into a conversation. But someone searching for Nikes can still get familiar shopping listings and text ads, says Andy Taylor, vice president of research at digital advertising agency Tinuiti. They’re looking to buy shoes rather than get an AI explanation of them.

Google’s argument is that AI is expanding the business. Schindler says most AI Mode queries are “incremental” — additional questions rather than replacements for existing searches. Many are also “upper-funnel,” meaning people are researching potential purchases before they know what they want. The company sees those searches as opportunities to sell more ads over time.

Google is currently expanding ads in AI Overviews and AI Mode, including internationally and across multiple turns of US AI Mode conversations. It has also floated bringing ads to its standalone Gemini app, saying formats developed for AI Mode could carry over, but hasn’t announced when.

The thing is, Google, as well as competitors like OpenAI, are still figuring out how to make ads work in AI — both delivering results for advertisers and avoiding alienating the people using their AI tools.

For now, advertisers are still getting results from Google. Tinuiti’s same-store advertisers increased their Google paid search spending 14 percent year over year in the third quarter while maintaining their return-on-ad-spend targets.

“If people are clicking through less often. That is a challenge that they need to figure out.”

“We’re still seeing a good return, and we’re not seeing performance take a hit,” Taylor says.

The catch is that advertisers can’t isolate how Google’s AI ads are doing. Google doesn’t let them separately target that inventory or break out its performance, Taylor says. Steady overall returns could mean AI ad traffic is still too small to noticeably affect the results, or that it performs similarly to existing inventory. Taylor suspects it’s mostly the former.

That leaves an important question unanswered: How much will AI add to Google’s advertising business, and how much could it replace? Additional queries could bring in new revenue even if each earns less than a traditional search. The risk is that AI Mode also draws people away from searches that Google monetizes more effectively. Google’s claim that most AI Mode queries are additional doesn’t tell us how much revenue it could lose from the searches AI Mode replaces.

“If people are clicking through less often,” Elliott says, “that is a challenge that they need to figure out.”

The promise of conversational advertising is that people share much more about their needs than they would in a few keywords. But platforms still have to identify which parts of a conversation matter and match them with the right ads.

“It’s not a slam dunk that it’s going to turn into more effective advertising,” Elliott says.

An early study of ChatGPT ads suggests richer conversations don’t automatically translate into more retail sales. An October working paper found that US visits from ChatGPT ads generated 64 percent less revenue for retailers per session than visits from Google Search ads: $0.89 versus $2.45. The working paper measures retailers’ sales rather than platform revenue, and its findings about ChatGPT don’t establish how Google’s own AI ads perform.

Taylor cautions that those comparisons depend on what people are searching for. Someone searching Google for Nike shoes may already be ready to buy, while someone asking an AI tool which running shoes to choose is still researching. A higher sales return could reflect that difference in purchase intent rather than more effective ads.

Google’s existing business has given it room to take its time with AI ads, Taylor says. He points to its successful shift to mobile: Early phone traffic was less valuable to advertisers because shopping on a phone was cumbersome, but that changed.

Elliott cautions that finding effective ad formats can take years. Search engines started with banner ads before adopting paid listings; social media went through its own experimentation. Today’s formats may not be the ones that ultimately work.

But Google’s mounting AI spending raises the stakes. Search revenue is still growing by double digits, but the company has yet to show how well its new way of searching can pay.

“They absolutely do not have it all figured out,” Elliott says. “No one has it all figured out.”

#210. What OPEC's surprise oil cut means for gas prices

OPEC and its allies’ surprise move to slash oil production will soon be felt at US gas pumps.

The group known as OPEC+ announced Sunday it would cut oil production by more than 1.6 million barrels a day starting in May, running through the end of the year. The news sent both Brent crude futures, the global oil benchmark, and WTI, the US benchmark, up about 6% in trading Monday.

The production cut announcement also had an immediate impact on gasoline futures, which will be passed onto US drivers much more quickly than the spike in oil prices. RBOB, the most closely watched wholesale gasoline price, was up about 8 cents a gallon, or about 3%, in morning trading.

“I think OPEC is reawakening the inflation monster,” said Tom Kloza, global head of energy analysis for OPIS, which tracks gas prices for AAA. “The White House has to be shocked and major-time pissed. It certainly alters the calculus for a while.”

The national average for US gas prices stood at $3.51, on Monday, according to AAA. Kloza said he could see it getting up to $3.80 to $3.90 in relatively short order thanks to the move by OPEC.

“We’re not going to get back to $5 a gallon. I don’t think we’re even going as high as $4,” he said. But he said by the end of the summer US drivers could be back above year-earlier prices, especially if there is a hurricane or other storms affecting production along the Gulf Coast.

The average US regular gas price a year ago stood at $4.19 a gallon in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and the disruption that caused to world’s energy markets. Prices eventually reached a record $5.02 a gallon on June 14, before starting a slow but steady decline over the course of more than three months during which the average price fell every day. The decline was partly driven by the release of oil from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve, and partly by concerns that there could be a US or global recession that reduced the demand for gasoline.

Oil prices surge after OPEC+ producers announce surprise cuts

Even at $3.51, US gas prices were just below the $3.53 average on Feb. 23, 2022, the day before Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

Kloza said one thing keeping prices from getting anywhere near the record levels of 2022 is that the US plans additional releases from the SPR, and US oil production and refining capacity are both up. But a cut of 1 million barrels a day of oil by OPEC+ will not be easy to make up.

“They have ability to cut production and they seem motivated to do so,” he said.

#209. Anthropic is working on a system to prove AI models’ identity

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Anthropic is working on an open source system to verify which AI model produced a given output, according to a blog post published Tuesday. The system, called FlashProve, is built to provide what researchers call “provable inference” — a cryptographic proof, essentially a mathematical guarantee, that a given output came from a specific model. It’s meant to let users confirm that the answer they received really came from the model they think they’re using.

Similar to cryptographic email signatures that let recipients confirm a message really came from its claimed sender, FlashProve would be robust against impostors attempting to disguise one model as another, even when the impostor is the lab that built and runs the model. In particular, the researchers note that the system could be used to prevent an AI company from secretly swapping in a cheaper model to cut costs, or a more expensive model to inflate its performance results.

FlashProve could also be used to verify claims by third parties that a specific task was done by an advanced model. “A developer might claim that their software library was scanned for vulnerabilities by a frontier cybersecurity model, or a company might claim that its business plan was given the go-ahead by an appropriate model,” the researchers write. “How can a user verify these claims?”

To work, the system requires AI labs to publish a “fingerprint” of each model they release that can later be matched to specific outputs and verified cryptographically. Crucially, the fingerprints don’t reveal a model’s weights, the internal numbers that define how it behaves and which closed-model makers like Anthropic and OpenAI keep private. That means labs could publish the fingerprints without giving up their most valuable asset, enabling the system to work with commercial models, too. The system is also designed to be lightweight, so verification could be performed on a local device.

As part of the announcement, researchers released a detailed technical specification and working code for various elements of the system, which is publicly available under an open source license on GitHub. Research in the system is ongoing, and there are no plans to deploy it at Anthropic or any other lab.

“This research post is meant to share intermediate progress, and not a completed research agenda,” researchers explain in the announcement post. “We are sharing this explicitly with the goal that others look at what we have done, take it, and improve it.”

#207. India’s EloElo nabs $80M from NBCUniversal to bet on the country’s rising microdrama market

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

More than 118 million users watch microdramas every month as the serialized format has taken off in India. EloElo’s Story TV is one of the top apps for microdramas, with over 33 million active users, according to analysts.

Banking on that scale, the company has raised $80 million in Series C from Hornbill Capital and NBCUniversal. This round brings its total fundraising to $130 million, with a valuation of $500 million.

As people spend more time on platforms hosting verticalized videos, serialized content has become popular in multiple geographies, including the U.S., China, and India, with Hollywood celebrities like Issa Rae, James Franco, and Kim Kardashian investing in and starring in the format.

Given India’s massive internet population of more than a billion and the country’s appetite for entertainment content, it presents a big market opportunity, with analysts expecting the industry to cross $3-$4 billion in annual revenue by 2030. Last year, dedicated apps like Story TV, Kuku TV, and Quick TV were among the top downloaded video streaming apps in the country.

Founded in 2020 by former Flipkart exec Saurabh Pandey, the Story TV platform has taken off in the last few years with a catalog of over 5,000 titles in six languages. Pandey told TechCrunch that because of the snackable content format, users log 95 minutes on the app on average through multiple sessions in a day.

“I fundamentally believe that the next billion users will probably not grow up on a diet of watching television; they will grow up on a diet of watching content on their phones, right? And this content has to be, and this entertainment, designed very differently. It’s not going to be appointment viewing. It’s going to be within certain slots throughout the day. And a lot of this was earlier solved for short clips, but serialized storytelling was never solved,” Pandey said about the microdrama format.

He believes that there still will be a place for big-screen viewing with family or friends, but the majority of entertainment consumption would be done through mobile.

All microdrama platforms compete against platforms like YouTube, Instagram, and TikTok, where creators are posting content for free. Some of them are also creating a way for creators to post serialized content. Pandey thinks that producing quality content on the platform will entice users to choose StoryTV over other free platforms. The company points to its partnerships, such as the one with Indian TV production company Balaji Telefilms, to produce content.

The company offers different kinds of subscriptions ranging from monthly, quarterly, and yearly to unlock content. It also offers some free content supported by ads, which might not be as lucrative a medium to make money as subscriptions, according to reports.

Monetization is a challenge in India, as users often stick around for free content, but few convert to paid users. A report from analyst firm Redseer indicates that more than 40 million people have paid for microdrama at least once, but only 15-17 million users pay for it on a monthly basis. A separate survey by Ormax Media suggested that 72% of people haven’t ever paid while consuming microdrama.

All microdrama platforms want to have enough supply to engage different audiences and want to involve AI to solve that. Some platforms, like Google-backed Kuku, want to improve workflows and create more content, and some platforms like Dashverse’s Shortfree want to use video AI models to create serialized clips.

At the moment, StoryTV falls into the first camp with its Dolphin AI platform. Started as an internal tool to improve video creation, the company is now planning to make the tool widely available. It can act as an editor with templates and an array of models to create images, audio, and video, according to its website.

EloElo’s vision is to have people use this tool for marketing campaigns and ads as well. Notably, while Indian platforms like Pocket and Kuku have built AI tools and given them to creators, it is likely to accelerate their content pipeline. But at InVideo, there hasn’t been a scaled effort to provide a good alternative to CapCut or Instagram Edits to creators from India.

Pandey believes that human involvement is still paramount to producing good stories, and AI tools are still creative directors for steering.

“My belief in involving AI is that AI is a really fast race car where AI is the engine, but you need an extremely capable Formula One driver to be on the steering wheel. That, for me, is the creative director or the showrunner. AI is supposed to work with them, augmenting their skill set. AI can help write stories, but so far the best stories I have loved have all had a very high human x AI element to it,” he said.

Besides microdrama, the company also has a vertical video learning app, Master, competing with the likes of Seekho and Kuku’s Guru, and an AI companion app, Voxy AI. The company said that it has more than 40 million monthly users across its apps. While it didn’t bifurcate users by app, the majority of users belong to Story TV.

EloElo currently has 240 people and wants to hire to increase the size of its AI and machine learning research team. The company is also deploying a good chunk of its capital to produce more original titles.

MIT Investment Co., along with existing investors like Play Ventures, Kalaari Capital, Waterbridge Ventures, and Griffin Gaming Partners, invested in the round.

#206. Canva lays off over 1,200 people amid slowing growth

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Design software maker Canva is cutting 22% of its staff, amounting to 1,271 people, amid a growth slowdown.

The company’s co-founder and CEO, Melanie Perkins, in a note to employees said while Canva has scaled so far, it needs to change to address slowing growth.

“Over the past thirteen years, we’ve turned a dream in a pitch deck into a platform used by hundreds of millions of people. None of it would have been possible without the talent, imagination, and care of every single person who has been part of this journey. Yet success in our first chapter doesn’t guarantee success in the next. This has been our hardest year yet, and as we shared earlier this year, our growth has slowed for the first time in a decade,” Perkins wrote.

Canva is reshuffling its organizational chart and merging its product, growth and platform teams to reduce communication friction. The company is also reducing “subspecialisation,” which means its employees might be asked to take up more responsibilities. The company is also moving towards hybrid work and asking people to work from an office a few days a week.

The company has been shipping its own AI models to reduce costs and reliance on third-party models, but it still had to cut its revenue growth forecast to 20% from 30% to address ballooning AI costs. This also resulted in Canva’s early investors cutting its valuation to about $35 billion earlier this year.

Canva is also facing increasing competition from AI startups and general-purpose chatbots like ChatGPT and Gemini, whose image generation capabilities allow people to easily put together posters and presentations.

#205. Trump blamed Fox News for Republican losses even as network’s hosts went easy on him

Fox News pundits hardly criticized the US president in midterms coverage but Trump was still quick to cast blame

Tell us: what do you want to know about the midterm election results?

In the weeks leading up to Tuesday’s midterm elections, Donald Trump made clear that he would place some blame on his favorite cable channel, Fox News, if his party did poorly.

Even before races really started to be called, Trump hit the network in multiple Truth Social posts, criticizing Fox for featuring Democratic pundit Jessica Tarlov; attacking Fox’s polls as “always the worst”; mocking some recent softness in the network’s year-over-year ratings trends and accusing Fox of being “AGAINST” him. Just before midnight, Trump directly placed blame on Fox News for the Republican party’s failure to hold the US House and Senate, writing that “FoxNews bears responsibility for these losses by constantly and consistently putting on the Dumocrat opposition, and worse.”

In a surreal moment, Trump then called into Fox’s election coverage around midnight and directly criticized the network’s polls as “terrible,” telling the network’s employees: “You ought to get yourself a good pollster, because your pollsters are the worst.” Fox News anchor Bret Baier pushed back gently, pointing out that “tonight, actually, the results are pretty close to what the Fox polls showed.” (Trump also made a dig about Baier – or Fox writ large –not supporting him “when it wasn’t fashionable”.)

But while Trump was extremely critical of Fox News throughout the day and night, during more than six and a half hours of election night coverage viewed by the Guardian, commentators on the network seemed to take pains to not directly tie the party’s losses to Trump, and there were no suggestions that the party should back away from him.

Instead, several commentators on the channel turned the focus on voters for being “impatient” and wanting “instant gratification”, as host Jesse Watters put it just after 8pm. Many commentators said the president’s party historically performs poorly in the midterm elections. There were many attacks on Democrats, with conservative commentators in agreement that the party would overstay their welcome from voters and misinterpret their victory as a mandate, paving the way for Republican wins in 2028.

The only significant criticisms of the president focused on the White House’s messaging strategy and his decision to go to war in Iran.

“In some sense, Mr Trump was too successful in the first year and didn’t sell it enough in the second year,” said Larry Kudlow, a Fox Business Network host who served as Trump’s Director of the National Economic Council during his first administration.

Dana Perino, who served as press secretary to George W Bush, said: “From a messaging standpoint, a lot of times the administration has got too many balls in the air and it’s hard to figure out what is the headline we want to drive.”

And several commentators noted that voters seemed to be unhappy with the president’s decision to launch a war against Iran, particularly considering the rise in gas prices that resulted from it. Sean Hannity, the president’s biggest booster on the channel, agreed with Baier that the night’s outcome would have been different if he had not bombed the country.

“I think it’s prices. People are mad,” Watters offered later in the night.

Still, the overwhelming consensus from Fox’s election night panel – which included several former Trump administration employees, including his most recent press secretary Karoline Leavitt – was that the blame should not be squarely placed at the president’s feet. Instead, Leavitt singled out the media. “Unfortunately, I think the American people forgot about the good policy, [and] maybe listened to the lying media on the other side that is definitely in the back seat of the Democratic party,” she said.

Just before 10pm, Fox News host Will Cain said: “I look at the pros and the cons of the last two years under Donald Trump, and the pros significantly outweigh the cons.”

While primetime host Laura Ingraham acknowledged that the election was a “referendum on Trump”, she said that “overwhelmingly his policies have been phenomenal for the country. Overwhelmingly, the stuff he’s promised, he’s done, and it’s worked out fabulously.”

Despite his earlier comments, Hannity said that Trump’s policies “are rock solid”, that Iran “had to be done” and that his intervention will eventually be proven right.

One person who shared in the belief that the president was not responsible for the losses was the president himself. “I don’t view it as a hit on me,” Trump told the Fox News panel, adding that “historically, I think I’ll be judged for having done the right thing,” and largely placing the blame on the Republican candidates who were defeated. He repeated his contention that the outcome would have been different if he had been on the ballot.

#203. Scientists film deepest ever fish on seabed off Japan

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Cruising at a depth of 8,336 meters (over 27,000 feet) just above the seabed, a young snailfish has become the deepest fish ever filmed by scientists during a probe into the abyss of the northern Pacific Ocean.

Scientists from University of Western Australia and Tokyo University of Marine Science and Technology released footage of the snailfish on Sunday filmed last September by sea robots in deep trenches off Japan.

Along with the filming the deepest snailfish, the scientists physically caught two other specimens at 8,022 meters and set another record for the deepest catch.

Previously, the deepest snailfish ever spotted was at 7,703 meters in 2008, while scientists had never been able to collect fish from anywhere below 8,000 meters.

“What is significant is that it shows how far a particular type of fish will descend in the ocean,” said marine biologist Alan Jamieson, founder of the Minderoo-UWA Deep Sea Research Centre, who led the expedition.

Scientists are filming in the trenches off Japan as part of a 10-year study into the deepest fish populations in the world. Snailfish are members of Liparidae family, and while most snailfish live in shallow water, others survive at some of the greatest depths ever recorded, Jamieson said.

During the two-month survey last year, three “landers” – automatic sea robots fitted with high-resolution cameras – were dropped into three trenches – the Japan, Izu-Ogasawara and Ryukyu trenches – at varying depths.

In the Izu-Ogasawara trench, footage showed the deepest snailfish hovering calmly alongside other crustaceans on the seabed.

Jamieson classified the fish as a juvenile and said younger deep sea snailfish often stay as deep as possible to avoid being eaten by bigger predators that swim at shallower depths.

Another clip shot at between 7,500 and 8,200 meters in the same trench showed a colony of fish and crustaceans munching at bait tied to an undersea robot.

Images of the two captured snailfish – identified as Pseudoliparis belyaevi – provide a rare glimpse of the unique features that help the deep sea species survive the extreme environment.

They have tiny eyes, a translucent body, and their lack of swim bladder, which helps other fish float, works to their advantage, Jamieson said.

The professor said the Pacific Ocean is particularly conducive to vibrant activity due to its warm southern current, which encourages sea creatures to go deeper, while its abundant marine life provides a good source of food for bottom feeders.

Scientists would like to know more about creatures living at extreme depths, but cost is the constraint, Jamieson said, adding that each lander alone costs them $200,000 to assemble and operate.

“The challenges are that technology has been expensive and scientists don’t have a lot of money,” he said.

#201. Election week in Trumpland: algae, space proclamations and a deflection of blame

Donald Trump’s unrelenting focus on the ‘beautification of Washington’ and Fox News ratings did not stop the Democrats’ decisive midterm election wins

This was originally published in This Week in Trumpland. Sign up to receive it in your inbox every Wednesday.

Standing at the Lincoln Memorial reflecting pool on Monday afternoon, Donald Trump zeroed in on his closing message of the high-stakes midterm elections: it can take a long time to get rid of filth.

Less than 24 hours before Americans headed to the polls, the US president staged a bizarre press conference in which he touted his troubled $14m renovation of the Washington DC landmark and blamed his predecessors for the algae that once bloomed in its water.

Holding up a blown-up photo of the pool when it was covered in algae, Trump said: “This was in the Biden era … They just spend money after money after money. Look, it’s not an easy thing. The algae is brutal. It forms easily … All you saw was dirt and filth and paper and everything else for years.”

It was a head-scratching event just one day before election day, particularly as polls have shown that Americans aren’t too pleased with the president’s intense focus on renovation projects. Never mind that the reflecting pool project itself was badly botched and became an international joke earlier this year when Trump’s promise to turn the water “American flag blue” ended with a green algae takeover.

The press briefing was yet another sign that Trump was struggling to read the room, with the Wall Street Journal reporting that he viewed “beautification of Washington” as one of his strongest legacies despite aides’ advice to focus on other matters.

Trump has not listened to such guidance. On one tangent at the reflecting pool, he praised his legacy, saying he had achieved more in two years than any other administration in history. “I’ve kept every single promise I made, except far more,” the president claimed, curiously adding: “I’ve actually kept far more promises than I made.”

By election day, Trump was turning to other priorities – publishing an “American declaration of freedom in space” which proclaimed “the solar system and all of outer space beyond Earth are the province of all humans”, and declaring this week “Anti-Communism Week” in honor of “the victims of socialism and communism”. It was unclear how the White House would be marking the occasion.

On Tuesday morning, as polls opened nationwide, Trump turned up on the rightwing Fox News network with a surprise announcement: he would end military aid to Israel at the end of the year, he claimed. “We were giving them $4bn (£2.96bn) … But it gets cut off at the end of the year,” he said by phone. It was a position more in tune with the views of voters, with a Quinnipiac poll in September finding nearly half of Americans believed the US was too supportive of Israel.

The president also said the quiet part out loud: he was sick of Democrats hammering him on the issue, citing the effective messaging of Abdul El-Sayed, the Senate candidate in Michigan who has criticized military aid to Israel. Trump said “the left” had used the $4bn figure as a “signal of unfairness, and we can’t allow that to happen”. “Why should we suffer the slings from lunatics like El-Sayed when they don’t need it? It’s a bad talking point,” he said.

By Tuesday evening, as polls began to close, the president was angrily ranting about Fox, the leading conservative news network that he long considered his ally, but which has increasingly drawn his ire.

“The FoxNews Poll, always the worst, is so insulting with the questions they ask,” he posted on his Truth Social website at 6.36pm. “Every question is a hit job, and every answer is ‘BULLSHIT!’”

Twenty minutes later, he posted again: “FoxNews Ratings are way down (27%!) because they’re spending too much time covering the Trump Deranged Dumocrats! All of those people who used to watch Fox, and no longer do, get it. It’s them, not me – My numbers are better than they have ever been.”

Within an hour, results started pouring in: major wins for Democrats in Georgia, New Hampshire and North Carolina.

As the bad news for Republicans continued at a rapid pace, signaling a stunning rebuke of the president, Trump issued a short statement with a simple message: It’s not his fault.

“I wasn’t on the Ballot and if I was, we would have won IN A LANDSLIDE!” he wrote on Truth Social, adding, “Thank you for your attention to this matter.”

#199. What pet cemeteries reveal about being human

Editor’s Note:  Eric Tourigny is a lecturer in historical archaeology at Newcastle University, UK. His research interprets osteological and material culture remains alongside historic texts to examine changing human-animal relationships in Europe and North America over the past 500 years. The views expressed in this commentary are his own. Read more opinion on CNN.

Why do we bury our departed loved ones in a cemetery? A primary purpose is to provide survivors with an opportunity to grieve and gain a sense of closure. The cemetery is a place for the living as much as it is a place for the dead.

But what about when the dearly departed are not human – but our pets?

The Spanish city of Barcelona recently announced that it will be investing in the establishment of the country’s first public pet cemetery. Set to open next year, it will offer both burials and cremations – with an estimated 7,000 carried out each year.

For me, as someone who has spent years researching the development of pet cemeteries elsewhere in the world, this news came as a shock. Barcelona is a densely populated city with limited privately owned land – one where 50% of families own a pet.

How did a city that is home to 180,000 dogs not already have a public pet cemetery? Until now, the service was only provided by the private sector, according to Barcelona’s Councilor for Climate Emergency and Ecological Transition, Eloi Badia. He added that the municipal-funded initiative was sparked by “constant public demand.”

After all, public pet cemeteries have been around in Europe and the Americas since the late 19th century. Britain’s first public pet cemetery appeared in London’s Hyde Park in 1881. New York’s Hartsdale pet cemetery was founded in 1896, followed a few years later by Paris’ ornate Cimetière des Chiens in 1899.

I became interested in the history of modern pet burial practices while investigating the archaeological record of a centuries-old house in Toronto. I came across a (very) large dog buried in the back garden which, according to the historical record, was occupied between 1840 and 1870.

This dog survived to an elderly age but, sadly, suffered from degenerative joint disease and severe infections during his last months. His ailments progressed to such a state that suggest he received some level of care in his final weeks. He then was buried in a personal plot behind the family home.

This elderly dog led me to think about the different ways people interact with the bodies of their pets after death. Could this behavior be reflective of the relationships they held with their animals in life? In this instance, why take the time to carefully bury a dog in its own space when other, arguably easier options existed?

This, after all, was an era when people often disposed of their dead pets in the river, or might have sold their bodies for meat and skin.

Good hygiene is an obvious reason to choose a burial – no one wants decomposing animal bodies in the street or their garden – but that wouldn’t immediately warrant a personalized, dedicated burial and gravestone.

The most straight forward option would be to dispose of a deceased animal with the household waste. But such treatment would evidently feel less ceremonious and would not offer appropriate emotional closure to what was likely an important relationship.

Like the burial of people, the burial of pets is an intimate cultural practice, one that changes over time and is reflective of the changing relationships that a society has with its cherished creatures.

My study of historic gravestones and epitaphs in Britain from the Victorian period to today shows this changing human-animal relationship. In the 19th century, gravestones were often dedicated to a “loving friend” or “devoted companion,” suggesting pets were mostly considered important friends.

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

Society’s changing attitude towards the role of animals in the afterlife can also be found. Fast-forward a few decades later, and gravestones were more likely to reference a reunion then earlier ones. For example, the owners of Denny, the “brave little cat” buried in an east London cemetery in 1952, wrote on his epitaph “God bless until we meet again.”

I wonder what the epitaphs in Barcelona’s new cemetery will reveal about modern Catalan relationships with animals.

Over time, our ways of treating our animal dead appear to reflect an even closer relationship in life. Once strictly forbidden by law, the last decade saw many jurisdictions, like the state of New York, allowing the co-burial of cremated animals and people, which will undoubtedly lead to changing funerary and commemoration practices for both humans and animals.

For me, the most remarkable similarity between modern and historic pet cemeteries is the striking evidence for the heartbreak and taboos around grieving for animals.

The connection that someone has with their pet can be just as strong and just as significant as their relationships with other humans. Yet today, as in over 100 years ago, individuals continue to struggle to find the appropriate outlet to express their pain, hiding heartache for fear of the social repercussions that might come with public acknowledgment of the existence of such a bond.

The RSPCA reassures the public on its website that they should not feel shame for their grief. In the UK, charities like the Blue Cross and Rainbow Bridge Pet Loss Grief Center offer counseling to bereaved humans.

Close relationships between people and animals have existed for millennia, but in western European cultures, there were few acceptable ways to mourn that relationship. As society becomes more accepting of the importance of human-animal relationships to our collective wellbeing, it is not surprising to see us follow rituals resembling those we use to mourn the loss of our closest human relationships.

At an estimated cost of €200 ($217) per service at the new Barcelona cemetery, it is important to acknowledge that this opportunity to grieve will not be financially available to everyone in the city.

This will not be a space for all of the city’s pets. Pet owners may opt to keep cremated remains within their home or spread out ashes in a meaningful location instead. Online forums and digital pet cemeteries also provide other opportunities to commemorate the relationship and express grief.

Whether one chooses a pet cemetery or not, there are many acceptable ways to express your grief – and to remember your relationship with the important animals in your life.

#198. Democrat Annie Andrews flips South Carolina Senate seat held by Republicans since 1998

Andrews ran a campaign on healthcare affordability and beat Darline Graham, sister of Lindsey Graham to win

Democrat Annie Andrews, a pediatrician who campaigned on healthcare affordability, has won the race for US Senate in South Carolina, making her the first Democrat to represent the state in the Senate for over a quarter century.

Andrews had faced an uphill battle in what has been seen as a reliably Republican state against Darline Graham, the sister of the late Republican powerbroker Lindsey Graham.

“I think we just made some history,” Andrews said to exuberant supporters in Charleston on Tuesday night at her victory party. “South Carolina, you have shown the nation what is possible. You have shown the nation that in this era of partisan divides, of vitriol and name calling, in a politics dominated by billionaires and special interest groups and insiders in Washington DC, that we will not bow down, that we will not back down, and we will chart our own course.”

Andrews called her candidacy a defense of South Carolina against external forces. “Tonight we here in South Carolina sent a clear message that this Senate seat does not belong to one family, one party, or to Washington DC”

The 62-year-old Graham, who had been appointed by South Carolina governor to serve out the remainder of her brother’s term after he died in July aged 71, had been publicly urged to run for office by Donald Trump, who enthusiastically campaigned for her and denigrated her Democratic opponent, including referring to Andrews using a transphobic slur.

But Graham’s lack of national political experience – she admitted she was “not that informed on national security” when asked about US policy on Taiwan during a debate in August – is believed to have made the race more competitive.

The last time South Carolina elected a Democrat to the Senate was in 1998, but a New York Times/Siena poll the week before the election had the two candidates neck and neck, both polling at 48%.

Graham had stayed close to Trump in her platform, including saying she fully trusts Trump on tariffs and the war with Iran, which is deeply unpopular with a majority of Americans.

Andrews, in contrast, had called the war with Iran “illegal” and criticized its effect on gas prices. Andrews said in September she wanted to “find our way out of this illegal, unconstitutional billion-dollar-a-day war overseas”.

Andrews had tried to separate herself from other Democrats on several issues, and faced criticism from LGBTQ+ advocates for an ad she aired in the final month of the campaign, in which she referred to trans women as “biological men” and said she doesn’t “support and haven’t ever performed gender surgery for minors”.

In 2022, Andrews had referred to South Carolina’s Save Women’s Sports Act as “actively harmful”, and wrote on X, “I will always support trans youth.”

The election marks the first time South Carolina has elected a woman to the senate, nearly a century after Hattie Caraway of Arkansas became the first female US senator, in 1932. As of October, 15 US states still have never had a woman as a US senator, including several liberal northeastern states, including Pennsylvania, Connecticut, New Jersey and Vermont.

Graham’s candidacy had been marred by a kind of political infighting among Republicans that makes the Haley Stevens’s defection in Michigan look like a scuffed shoe. Outgoing US representative Ralph Norman pointedly refused to endorse Graham, describing her as unqualified for the role, and a write-in campaign for Norman emerged to tempt the arch-conservative’s voters.

Graham said during her concession speech that she owed a debt of gratitude to Trump for his endorsement and that the last few months had been difficult, referring both to her candidacy and the loss of her brother.

“I love this state with every fiber of my being, and I believe our state and nation’s best days are ahead of us,” Graham said. “I’ve run in a contentious primary and runoff extending into mid-August and now a general election. I will trade this all 1,000 times over to have my brother back, but God had a different plan. I cannot begin to tell you how proud I am to have our party’s nominee and how hard our team fought, and I am so proud.”

#196. US midterms voters said: enough is enough. Is this the beginning of the end for Trump?

With Democrats in power, Trump may be under siege and lose his dominance over the Republican party – but his id will relish the prospect of fighting

Tell us: what do you want to know about the midterm election results?

Donald Trump posted a document on social media this summer by a supposed historian that compared him with, among others, Napoleon Bonaparte and Adolf Hitler. That now seems fitting: the US president has himself fought one battle too many, and appears to have brought about his own destruction.

Tuesday’s US midterm elections were a thumping, shellacking and extinction-level event rolled into one. Ruby-red Republican strongholds once deemed safe toppled like dominoes. Democrats won Senate seats for the first time in Kansas since 1932, in Texas since 1988 and in South Carolina since 1998.

The House of Representatives and Senate had flipped from one party to the other in the same midterm just three times since the second world war – in 1946, 1994 and 2006 – but now it has happened again, ensuring that the US Congress will be a bulwark against Trump’s expansion of executive power.

The president, who swiftly noted he was not on the ticket on Tuesday, is the architect of his own lame duck status. Indeed, having spent months repeatedly urging voters to pretend he was on the ticket, he could hardly have done any more to sabotage his own party. In 2024 he won the presidency promising to lower prices and end foreign wars; his tariffs added to upward pressure on prices, and his ill-starred attack on Iran pushed them higher still.

Then there was Trump’s wild and bizarre campaign, which consisted of long and rambling speeches about architectural projects, outbursts of vile Islamophobia and musings about the “ass” of a Republican senator from Ohio. It culminated at the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool, where he declared victory in the war on algae.

Now for the Republican postmortem. According to exit polls from the Associated Press, voters said the cost of living and government corruption were their two top issues. Trump, who was purported to be an economic populist, had flaunted his self-enrichment and fancy ballroom even as people struggled to put food on the table. It was too much even for some in his supposedly rock-solid base.

How will Trump cope with this new political reality? Is this the beginning of the end?

From January, Democrats will have the power to block legislation, launch investigations into Trump’s administration and thwart his judicial and senior government appointments. Life could suddenly become uncomfortable for the likes of Pete Hegseth, the defense secretary, or Big Tech leaders such as Elon Musk. Democrats might even consider impeaching Trump for a third time.

That potentially leaves the president under siege – a diminished figure mired in Washington gridlock and reduced to wielding a veto pen, which he had already starting threatening to use on Tuesday evening.

However, it must be said that Trump paid little attention to Congress during his first two years in office, apart from the passage of his Big, Beautiful Act of tax and spending measures. He has acted unilaterally on tariffs and military strikes on Venezuela and Iran. Like his predecessors, he will surely continue to pursue foreign policy as a legacy project, an alarming prospect for the world.

He will also push on with his other obsession: giving Washington a radical makeover with a new White House ballroom, a triumphal arch and more gold. Congress might take the view that this is a relatively harmless activity to keep him occupied compared to the havoc he could be wreaking elsewhere.

More threatening to Trump than any Democratic investigation is the inexorable march of time. The race to succeed him in 2028 begins now.

This president is psychologically ill-equipped to deal with the prospect of the national spotlight leaving him to focus on JD Vance, Marco Rubio and other potential candidates.

That shift could also eat away at Trump’s cult-like dominance of the Republican party and the Maga movement. He has shaped the party in his own image for a decade and it will not easily snap back. But members of Congress picking through the wreckage of Tuesday may well conclude that Trump is a creature of the past – and their survival depends on placing the right bets on the future.

Every swing of the electoral pendulum gets over-interpreted. Trump’s two presidential election victories were seen as a fundamental shift in America, even though he never achieved 50% of the national popular vote. His defeat in 2020 was hailed as the end of the Trump era, only for him to rise from the political dead four years later.

Anyone re-proclaiming the end of that era after Tuesday’s landslide should remember that the president is not yet halfway through his second term, and that his id will relish the prospect of fighting Democrats.

A wounded Trump who feels he has nothing to lose over the next two years might turn out to be the most reckless and dangerous of all.

#195. US midterm election results: Democrats flip House and Senate. Here's what else the data tells us

Democrats have won the majority in both House and Senate. Vote counts are still ongoing. DW takes a look at who voted Republican, who voted Democrat and what it all means.

This article was updated throughout the night. DW follows the Associated Press (AP) for when a seat is called for Republicans or Democrats.

In the 2026 midterms, Americans voted on all 435 members of the House of Representatives and 35 members of the Senate. Before election night, Republicans held the majority in both Congress chambers, if only by a hair.

Midterm elections are held every four years. The results will influence US politics for the next few years. Despite the significant executive power delegated to the presidency according to the US Constitution, the majority of federal political decisions have to be approved by Congress before they can be implemented.

For ongoing updates, go to our US midterms live blog.

In the US Senate, Democrats have managed to flip several Republican seats, winning races in Republican strongholds such as North Carolina, South Carolina, Kansas, Texas, and Iowa. With that, they have crossed the majority-line of 51 seats required to gain control of the chamber.

In the House of Representatives, all 435 seats are up for election. Democrats have crossed the threshold of 218 seats required to win the majority. That means they have flipped both chambers of Congress.

The data on voter demographics and attitudes is based on a representative survey of almost 80,000 verified voters conducted by survey and market research firm SSRS on behalf of AP and other news organizations. It builds on interviews of both early and mail-in voters as well as exit polls of in-person voters on Election Day. Where percentages of Republican and Democratic voters don't add up to 100%, the remaining percent were voters who voted for third-party candidates.

Republicans continue to make up the majority of white male voters. 55% of white men polled voted Republican. On the opposite end of the spectrum are Black women, 92% of whom voted Democrat. Remarkably, the majority of both Latino men and women voted Democrat in this election. Two years ago, Latinos were a big part of the vote that carried Trump to his second term in the White House.

The data shows that Republicans have lost support among all demographic groups compared to the last midterms in November 2022. Even among white men, the share of those who voted Republican fell from 60% to 55%. Among white women, the Republicans' share fell from 56% to 47%, and among Latinos, the percentage of Republican voters decreased as well.

Age-wise, Democrats are strongest among young voters. Three-quarters of 18- to 24-year-olds voted Democrat. On the flipside, Republicans lead with 51% among voters aged 65 and up.

The urban-rural divide that has been part of US politics for years continues to hold. Among urban voters, 71% voted Democrat, among suburban voters, that number stands at 58%. On the other hand almost 60% of rural and small town voters were Republicans.

Democrats make up 62% of voters with a college degree. Among voters without a college degree, the split is less significant: 47% voted Democrat and 51% Republican.

One of the most important factors in this year's midterms is the economy. Prices for gas have increased significantly since the start of the Iran war, and everyday goods have become more and more expensive across the country as well. Almost half of all Americans say their family's financial situation is worse than two years ago.

That sentiment is not the same across the board though. Among those saying their family is better off than two years ago, 75% voted Republican and 24% Democrat. Among those saying they are worse off, only 24% voted Republican and 74% Democrat. President Trump has been saying that the country is on the upswing.

On the question of which issue facing the country is most crucial, voters are divided among party lines. Of those who said immigration is the most important issue, 89% voted Republican and only 10% voted Democrat. Anti-immigrant rhetoric has been a mainstay of Trump's speeches. He promised to ramp up deportations at the start of his second term. Many states have seen a sharp increase in activity from federal immigration agencies. ICE (Immigration and Customs Enforcement) agents have been arresting immigrants off the street, even people who have ongoing asylum application processes, for example. Trump and his supporters say these actions are necessary to make the US safer.

Among those who say that the state of democracy is the most pressing issue in the US, 78% voted Democrat. Congressional Democrats and their supporters have been saying they are concerned about how Trump ignores direct judicial orders, for example to halt deportations, or Congressional resolutions. Among those saying the economy is the most important issue, there's an almost even split of Republican and Democratic voters.

American voters are split on major foreign policy decisions made by the Trump administration. Among those saying that the joint US-Israeli war against Iran was the right decision, 91% were Republican and only 7% Democratic voters. Among those saying it was the wrong decision to attack Iran, 82% were Democratic voters and 16% were Republican voters.

The US support for Israel has been a highly controversial topic ever since Israel began its strikes on Gaza in retaliation to the Hamas terror attack on Israel on October 7, 2023. The two countries have long been partners, with the US being the most important ally for Israel, and Jewish voters a crucial demographic in the US. But as the humanitarian situation in Gaza deteriorates, support for Israel among the US population, particularly among younger people and liberals, has been wavering. Of those saying US support for Israel is too strong, 78% were Democrat voters and 20% were Republican voters. Among those saying support is about right, only one quarter was Democrat voters.

Among those dissatisfied with the House's and Senate's work, one third are Republican voters. With the extreme level of polarization in the US, reaching compromises to pass laws or approve a budget in Congress has become harder in recent years. That could be contributing to dissatisfaction.

Of people seeing their country as going in the right direction in general, only 4% were Democrat voters. Of those saying they're dissatisfied or even angry about the US's current direction, 70% were Democrat voters — and 27% Republican.

92% of those who said they approved of ICE's actions were Republican, while of those who disapproved, 86% were Democrat. Regarding Trump's tariff policy, there's a split along party lines as well, with Republican voters largely feeling they helped the economy. The president had introduced sharply  increased tariffs on goods from a large number of US trading partners. Trump said the move was intended to even out unfair trade balances and protect American manufacturing. He has also used tariffs to punish countries he saw as acting against his political interests. The trade measures have made many goods more expensive for Americans.

Trump is not on the ballot, but the midterms are considered a referendum on how he and his Republicans governed the country for the past two years. Most recently, Trump's approval ratings have fallen to the lowest he has ever had, with just under 30% of Americans saying they think he is doing a good job.

In recent history, only George W. Bush has fared worse, with 25% at the end of his second term. During the 2006 midterm election, Bush's Republican Party lost control of both chambers of Congress.

#194. CEOs are tired of being held responsible for gun regulation

Americans have grown used to corporate executives treading the well-worn paths of the Northeast corridor to convene alongside elected officials in Washington, DC, and discuss geopolitics, policy and all that’s in-between.

In 2017, major CEOs from across the country came together to oppose North Carolina’s transgender bathroom law. In 2019, they called abortion bans “bad for business.”

After the deadly attack on the US Capitol on January 6, 2021, many of corporate America’s biggest names denounced the rioters and pledged to halt their political giving.

Recently, more than 1,000 companies promised to voluntarily curtail their operations in Russia in protest of Moscow’s war on Ukraine.

Dick’s Sporting Goods stopped selling semi-automatic, assault-style rifles at stores and Citigroup put new restrictions on gun sales by business customers after the mass shooting at a high school in Parkland, Florida, in 2018.

A year later, after mass shootings at a Walmart in El Paso, Texas, and a nightclub in Dayton, Ohio, Walmart ended handgun ammunition sales.

Corporate leadership has long been vocal on the issue of gun control – in 2019 and again this past summer nearly 150 major companies – including Lululemon, Lyft, Bain Capital, Bloomberg LP, Permanente Medical Group and Unilever – called gun violence a “public health crisis” and demanded that the US Senate pass legislation to address it.

That’s why corporate America’s silence in the wake of the latest mass shooting at a school in Nashville is so jarring. The United States has come to rely on the increasing power of large corporations as political advocates.

But Yale professor Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, a vocal advocate of corporate social responsibility who has a direct line to major CEOs around the globe, said that top executives are forlorn. Their previous efforts haven’t done much to push the needle on gun control legislation and without more backing, they don’t know what else they can do at the moment, he said.

Before the Bell spoke with Sonnenfeld, who runs Yale School of Management’s Chief Executive Leadership Institute, a nonprofit educational and research institute focused on CEO leadership and corporate governance.

This interview has been edited for clarity and length.

Before the Bell: CEOs have been quiet about gun reform since the latest mass school shooting in Nashville, have you heard anything about plans to speak out?

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld: Where is everybody else? Where is all of civil society? CEOs are just one group of people and it’s like we’re turning to them to be our saviors on every topic. They’ve joined causes with valor and nobility but they can’t just be taking cause after cause as if there’s nobody else in society. The social change that happened in the 1960s wasn’t being led primarily by CEOs. Social changes really happened when we saw the interfaith activity of clergy locking arms and canvassing legislators. We saw campuses alive and aroused. Where’s all the student activism?

The CEOs are still the most active even if they’re less active than they were six months ago. They’re not there as hired hands of shareholders to fill the role of politicians and civic leaders. They’re there to join that chorus, but they don’t want to be the only one singing.

So is this what you’re hearing from top CEOs? Have they gotten tired of advocating?

I just got off of a CEO call on voting rights and this morning we had a forum on sustainability – CEOs are still the most active on these fronts. It’s the same thing on immigration reform. If a CEO was working an 18 hour day on a 12 day week, they still couldn’t address all of the issues that need addressing.

The nation’s CEOs are waiting for everybody else to join them. They don’t need to restate something they’ve already stated. They’ve jumped in the pool, where’s everybody else?

So what do you think has led to this complacency amongst Americans and the growing reliance on CEOs to advocate on our behalf?

They’ve taken a very strong stance and they’ve gone out further than the general public. They are where the general public is on surveys, but they’re not where the general public is on action in the streets. So we’re ready for others to now do something. Enough already on saying ‘what are the CEOs doing?’ Social capital is as valuable as financial capital. CEOs understand that in their soul, they want there to be social capital. They want there to be public trust, but they need the rest of civil society to join them. And that’s their frustration.

But don’t these CEOs hold the purse strings in terms of donating to powerful politicians?

You would think that, but since the 2020 elections much less of campaign contributions have come from big business. Since the 2021 run on the Capitol, a lot of businesses either had an official moratorium or they’ve given mere pennies to politicians. The common impression on the street that CEOs are controlling campaign purses strings is 100% wrong.

Tesla reported. a modest 4% rise in sales in the first quarter compared to the final three months of last year, despite a series of price cuts on its lower priced vehicles and talk by CEO Elon Musk about strong demand at those lower prices.

The first quarter also marked the fourth straight quarter that Tesla has produced more vehicles than it has delivered to customers. Some of that may be due to the ramp up in production at two new factories, one in Texas, the other in Germany, which opened last spring, and a lag between that increased production and sales.

Tesla said there was an increase in the number of its more expensive models, the Model S and Model X, in transit to Europe, the Middle East and Africa, as well as to the Asia Pacific region.

But it does mean that over the last 12 months Tesla has produced 78,000 more cars than it has sold, suggesting that talk of strong demand by Tesla executives may not be backed up by the numbers.

“Early this year, we had a price adjustment. After that, we actually generated a huge demand, more than we can produce, really,” said Tom Zhu, Tesla’s executive in charge of global production and sales. “And as Elon said, as long as you offer a product with value at affordable price, you don’t have to worry about demand.”
