#191. US midterm elections 2026 live updates: Democrats take control of Congress in major setback for Trump

Democrats flip seven Senate seats in Kansas, Iowa, Texas, Maine, Ohio, North Carolina and South Carolina; ‘blue wave’ rejects Trump’s agenda

Full report: Democrats win control of Congress in ‘blue wave’

Tell us: how do you feel about the US midterm election results?

Tell us: what do you want to know about the midterm election results?

Hello and welcome to the US politics live blog.

In case you fell asleep early, yesterday evening resonated as an extinction level-event for Donald Trump’s Republican Party, a worst-case scenario for the GOP that even the starkest polls might’ve underestimated.

Democrats defended a crucial Senate seat in Michigan and won the likely pickups in Maine and North Carolina. They then proceeded to flip five more seats in states that would normally be considered unquestionably red: Ohio, Iowa, Kansas, South Carolina, and — the biggest prize of them all — Texas.

As of Wednesday morning, Democrats have secured 54 seats in the Senate. A closely-watched race in Alaska is yet to be called. And the Senate contest in ruby red Mississippi is down to the wire, with Democrat Scott Colom leading by just a few dozen votes as the final ballots roll in.

The Democrats have already secured 228 seats in the House, ten more than needed for a majority, and could realistically run up the score to 240 seats or more. They’ve also flipped governor’s offices in Texas, Georgia, Iowa, Ohio, and Nevada.

Republicans, needless to say, are shell-shocked, with the future of the party deeply uncertain, and some sounding the death-knell for a decade of Trump-dominated GOP politics.

Here are some other developments you may have missed last night:

Trump, uncharacteristically gracious, accepted his party’s resounding defeat, congratulated Democrats, and told The New York Times that he was “absolutely willing to accept” the election results. That was very much in doubt after the Trump administration spent months seeking to exert greater influence over the elections process through executive orders and the courts.

Deb Haaland, running in New Mexico, became the first Native American woman elected as governor. Keisha Lanca Bottoms became the first Black woman ever elected governor. Abdul El-Sayed, the victorious Democratic Senate candidate in Michigan, became the first Muslim voted into the upper chamber.

Several key congressional races, including contests in Colorado, Michigan, Maine, New York, California, and North Carolina, remain to be called.

Former World Series champion turned Republican politician Mark Teixeira will represent ⁠Texas’s 21st Congressional District in Washington after defeating Democrat Kristin Hook in Tuesday’s midterm elections.

On Wednesday morning, Teixeira was leading with 54.5% ⁠of the ​votes after 99% of the ballots had been counted.

“Tonight’s victory belongs ⁠to the people of Texas’s 21st District. I’m not going to Washington for myself, but rather to be a strong, responsive ⁠voice for every family in this district,” wrote on X. “Together, we can build a ​better future for our families, ‌our neighbors, and every community ‌across Texas 21. I’m excited to get to work. I will ‌be praying for all of you and for the future of this great nation. God bless Texas, and God bless America.”

Teixeira’s victory was expected. The seat has been in Republican hands since 1979. It was previously held by Chip Roy, who didn’t run for reelection. The district includes deeply conservative parts of Texas in the Austin ‌and San Antonio areas.

Donald Trump and Texas’s Republican governor, Greg Abbott, endorsed Teixeira’s campaign. Abbott lost his ​reelection campaign against Democrat Gina Hinojosa, and the Republicans suffered sweeping losses across the US in a repudiation of Trump’s second term in power.

According to Teixeira’s campaign website he aims to “end radical woke indoctrination, defend parental rights, kill DEI, restore patriotic education rooted in American and Texas values … and fight against Cultural Marxism.”

Teixeira, 46, was born and raised in Maryland, and he attended Georgia Tech. A first-round draft pick by the Rangers in 2001, he played for Texas ⁠from 2003-07 before stints with the Atlanta Braves (2007-08), Los Angeles Angels (2008) ​and New York ​Yankees (2009-16). Teixeira was selected an All-Star ​in 2005, 2009 and 2015, and he helped the Yankees ​win the 2009 ‌World Series. ​

Cindy Hyde Smith, Mississippi’s Republican Senator who is at risk of losing her seat in a seismic upset, may be paying the price for a particularly damaging remark she made to a local news outlet.

Questioned on a regional television network about how Mississippians should cope with the rising price of beef under the Trump administration, Hyde Smith responded by musing: “you know, you have so many proteins to choose from.”

Hyde Smith’s challenger, Democrat Scott Colom, seized on the comment in a campaign ad, which landed just as affordability was taking center stage as an issue in the 2026 midterms. Instead of picking another protein, the ad insisted, Mississippi voters should “pick another Senator.”

It remains to be seen whether Mississippi voters will take Colom up on the offer. But they’ve already come far closer to doing so than anybody expected going into Tuesday’s election.

Lauren Boebert, the hard-right Colorado congresswoman who has been one of Donald Trump’s most outspoken allies in the House of Representatives, is at risk of losing her seat in a close race with her Democratic challenger.

With more than 80 percent of the vote in, Boebert trails by one-and-a-half percent in the race for Colorado’s 4th District to Eileen Laubacher, a onetime Navy rear admiral and former Republican who switched parties.

Amid a slate of devastating losses for President Trump’s, Boebert’s ouster would nonetheless represent the rebuke of a congresswoman who was emblematic of the kind of lawmaker Trump sought to cultivate: hard-right, provocative, and fiercely loyal to the Trump brand.

The results of a closely-fought California ballot proposal to impose a one-time tax on billionaires remain too close to call as of Wednesday morning, the Associated Press reported.

If it passes, the measure would hit the state’s ultra-wealthy with a one-time 5% tax. It prompted something of an internecine war among California Democrats, winning support from top progressives, but getting blowback from some party leaders including California Gov. Gavin Newsom, who fear its passage would force an exodus of the state’s ultra-wealthy.

Those same ultra-wealthy have also spent lavishly to campaign against the proposal.

#187. Firework noise limit should be drastically cut to protect wild animals, UK government told

Ministers urged to go further than proposed cut to 110 decibels to ease acute stress on birds and others animals

Campaigners are calling on the UK government to protect the welfare of wild animals by cutting the maximum noise level of fireworks sold to the public from 120 to 90 decibels.

The Center for Wild Animal Welfare (CWAW) has said lowering the limit to 90dB – which is about one-eighth of the loudness of 120dB – would help to protect millions of wild animals from acute stress by reducing the area over which a firework disturbs surrounding wildlife.

This would help prevent incidents of harm, such as mass temporary displacement and fatal collisions among birds, that can occur during fireworks displays, it says.

The government recently consulted on proposals to lower the maximum noise level of fireworks sold to the public from 120dB to 110dB, which equate to their loudness being approximately halved.

But CWAW said the proposed limit did not go far enough and that cutting it to 90dB would leave the area disturbed by each firework about 100 times smaller than under the government’s plan.

Richard Parr, the CWAW’s director, said: “Sound travels, and a loud firework can penetrate surprisingly far – up to 10km. Because decibels work on a [logarithmic] scale, each 10-decibel cut to the limit reduces the area affected by the noise by 10 times.”

Parr said that while many people were aware of the distress caused by fireworks to pets, there was not enough awareness of how such loud noises affected wild animals. “There’s no reason to think that wild animals would be any less affected than pets,” he said.

“It might even be worse because it’s completely unnatural. It’s surprising, stressing, and disturbing for their natural rhythms of life to be suddenly and very unexpectedly disrupted. Particularly for prey animals, who have evolved to be cautious and are naturally jumpy, these random, unannounced bangs are terrifying.”

Rebecca Machin, a wildlife expert at the RSPCA, said she shared the CWAW’s concerns about the impact of fireworks on wildlife. “Fireworks and their debris can disturb a range of wild animals, particularly birds, causing suffering and distress,” she said. “Depending on the distance from the explosive and the noise level, impacts include disrupted migration, elevated stress hormones, and hazards from leftover firework debris.”

Research into the impact of fireworks on wild animals has found that birds experience particular harm during fireworks displays, including stress responses, mass temporary displacement and fatal collisions. On New Year’s Day in 2025, more than 1,000 birds were found dead near a hiking trail in Bulgaria, just over a kilometre from a site where fireworks had been set off, after panicked birds flew into trees and each other.

The government consultation came after concerns about the noise and availability of fireworks were aired in a parliamentary debate in January, with two petitions gaining more than 180,000 signatures each. Alongside the noise limit, the consultation looked at expanding the list of fireworks banned from general sale and reviewing the rules on lower-risk products, including scrapping the age limit on Christmas crackers.

New Zealand introduced a 90dB limit on consumer fireworks in 2008. It also reduced the number of days they could be sold and raised the minimum purchase age from 14 to 18, in line with the UK and most other countries.

The UK government consultation noted there had been concerns that the noise testing system could be gamed because a 90dB limit was difficult to define precisely in legislation.

In response to the calls for a 90dB limit, a spokesperson for the British Fireworks Association said: “We are aware of reports that sudden, unexpected noise from fireworks and other sources can disturb birds and other wildlife, and we take those concerns seriously. People should consider where and when they set off fireworks and take steps to minimise disturbance.

“However, we do not believe the case has been made for reducing the noise limit to 90 decibels. Any change should be based on clear evidence of its likely benefits and take account of unintended consequences, including the risk of people turning to illegal fireworks. We favour better enforcement of existing laws, alongside a national campaign to encourage safe and considerate use of fireworks.”

#184. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#183. What US midterm elections mean for Ukraine, Iran and elsewhere

Donald Trump's Republicans will no longer control the US Congress after the Democrats flipped both chambers in the midterm elections. The outcome could impact conflicts in Europe and the Middle East.

The outcome of the midterm election will shape policy in the United States for years and have repercussions around the globe. Decisions made in Washington affect both US adversaries such as Iran and countries such as Ukraine, which is seeking more US support.

President Donald Trump does not have the final say when it comes to deciding where the United States goes to war or which countries get financial or military aid. While the president wields more power in the US than in parliamentary democracies such as Germany, he does not govern alone — even in the land of opportunity.

The House of Representatives and the Senate, the two chambers of Congress in which the Democratic Party secured a clear majority on Tuesday, wield considerable influence. They decide on legislation and government spending and also serve as a check on the president and the intelligence agencies.

The president can veto legislation passed by Congress. Congress can override that veto with a two-thirds majority in both chambers. The legislature also handles impeachment proceedings. In addition, the president can only appoint Cabinet members, US ambassadors and federal judges from a list of nominees the Senate approves.

In the 2024 US election, the Republican Party secured not just the White House through Trump's reelection but also both chambers of Congress, which made it easier for the US president to govern.

The Democrats' resounding midterm victory has now flipped control of both chambers, which could shift the direction of major foreign policy issues.

Joe Biden, a Democrat, was in the White House when Russia launched its invasion of Ukraine. Alongside Europe, the US stood out as a reliable partner, supporting Ukraine with financial aid, military expertise and, above all, large-scale weapons deliveries.

The situation changed abruptly under Trump. Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy  famously received a notably frosty welcome during his 2025 visit to the White House. Trump now openly admits that he gets along better with Russian President Vladimir Putin than with Zelenskyy.

The US has suspended sanctions against Russia and reduced financial and military aid to Ukraine. Now the Democrats have majorities in both chambers of Congress, they could put increased support for Ukraine on the agenda.

"Democrats criticize the Trump administration for not doing enough for Ukraine," Brandon Conradis, a former DW journalist and now an editor at Bloomberg Government, told DW before Tuesday's election. "They can push legislation that would provide more aid to Ukraine."

If Trump vetoed such legislation, the outcome would depend on the balance of power in Congress. But a two-thirds majority could override his veto with support from some Republicans.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Trump launched the war against Iran alongside Israel on February 28, 2026, without consulting Congress. Under the US Constitution, Congress must approve a formal declaration of war against another country. Democrats have repeatedly voted against the war and even passed a resolution, together with some Republicans, calling for an end to the hostilities. The Trump administration has ignored it.

"I don't think Congress will have huge powers here," said Michael O'Hanlon, director of research for foreign policy at the US think tank Brookings Institution, told DW before the midterm elections, but added that Congress could perhaps prevent a major escalation by refusing to fund it.

US support for aid projects around the world that help fight Ebola, AIDS and hunger was sharply cut back by President Trump shortly after he began his second term. Among other measures, he shut down USAID, the US agency responsible for foreign development assistance.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Whether a Democratic-controlled Congress could "reactivate" the agency against the president's wishes is questionable. But Congress could push for increased aid spending and investigate the controversial closure of USAID.

"Democrats would have oversight power and use it to scrutinize the closing of USAID," Conradis said. "There will be hearings, and they will drag officials they view as responsible in front of Congress."

Ultimately, the US remains a country with a Republican president. There will be no complete reversal, of course. "It's not like if Democrats win control of Congress, everything is going to change. But some things will change because the power dynamics will be different."

This article was originally published in German on November 3, 2026. It was updated on November 4, 2026, to reflect the outcome of the midterm election.

#181. UK to deploy facial recognition in biggest-ever immigration crackdown

Documents show Home Office developing programme to use technology for general immigration enforcement

The Home Office is preparing to deploy facial recognition technology at transport hubs and public events as part of the biggest immigration enforcement crackdown in British history.

Policy documents show the government has been developing a programme to deploy live facial recognition (LFR) cameras for “general immigration enforcement”, including non-criminal immigration offences.

The technology will be used to identify people “seeking to re-enter the UK unlawfully”, target those subject to removal action and to crack down on broader “immigration-related criminality”.

The Home Office states the cameras will be used to locate undocumented people so it can resolve their cases, which it says will reduce “disruption to the wider public compared to repeated enforcement visits at residential addresses or workplaces”.

The documents further suggest that people could be included on LFR watchlists for overstaying a visa without permission, or entering without leave, all of which are considered civil rather than criminal matters.

The developments are contained in a new guidance document for immigration enforcement officials published on 8 October and a data protection impact assessment made public later in the month.

Their publication follows the decision by Shabana Mahmood, the home secretary, to double the budget for immigration enforcement and boost its workforce by 60% to allow “tens of thousands” more raids, arrests and deportations.

Between November last year and February, immigration enforcement conducted three trials of LFR technology at Holyhead port in north Wales.

The trials were narrowly focused on locating people travelling to the UK from Ireland through the common travel area in breach of deportation orders or on a warrant for “an immigration-related criminal offence”.

The documents suggests that, in the future, individuals “liable to detention under immigration legislation” and who have “failed to comply with immigration conditions of entry or stay” could also be put on a watchlist. The LFR project “has now moved into the formal delivery phase”, it is said.

Locations where immigration enforcement could deploy facial recognition cameras have also been expanded to include “proactive” deployments at transport hubs and at “specific” events which are “expected to increase footfall at a location”, although no specific geography is named.

A list of potential higher-risk deployments includes locations with “heightened community sensitivity”. When the Home Office was asked for clarity on what was meant by “events” or “sensitive” locations, it refused to comment.

A spokesperson confirmed that it was “considering further trials” of LFR by immigration enforcement officers and that it was “actively exploring the potential benefits, implications and future use of the technology”.

The new policy document states no one under the age of 18 will be included on a watchlist. Earlier this year it was revealed that the Home Office had continued to use an AI facial-age estimation tool despite knowing it had misidentified child migrants as adults.

Our morning email breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

Campaigners have called the “secretive” expansion a “huge and shocking escalation of mass surveillance” and highlighted the well-documented bias towards misidentifying people of colour when used at some settings.

Griff Ferris, a spokesperson for the Joint Council for the Welfare of Immigrants, said immigration enforcement already employed tactics that “persecute and terrorise migrant communities” and that giving it more power to use a “racist mass surveillance tool in public places is frightening”.

Sarah Lasoye, of the Open Rights Group, said immigration enforcement “appear to be giving themselves the green light to expand use of highly controversial facial recognition technologies without scrutiny, transparency or public consent”.

She said: “Immigration enforcement and police forces are exploiting an absence of national legislation regulating uses of AI technologies, including facial recognition, in the public sector.”

The policy documents claim the use of LFR by immigration enforcement “is subject to external oversight, including by the biometrics and surveillance camera commissioner and the Information Commissioner’s Office”.

The biometrics and surveillance camera commissioner, William Webster, told Liberty Investigates, which first uncovered the new policy documents, that he was not consulted on the policy and clarified that the use of LFR for immigration purposes fell outside his remit.

He added that the “proliferation of LFR into different service contexts does raise important questions about the scope of existing regulatory oversight”, which he hopes will be addressed in the forthcoming legislative framework for facial recognition.

#179. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#175. M&S boss bemoans last week’s budget as ‘whole lot of nothing’

Stuart Machin hits out at Labour but welcomes expected UK-EU food deal as retailer reveals half-year results

The chief executive of Marks & Spencer has called John Healey’s first budget as chancellor a “whole lot of nothing” and bemoaned an “onslaught of taxation and regulation” from the Labour government despite welcoming an expected deal on EU border checks.

Stuart Machin said he was disappointed at last week’s budget statement, adding that it “didn’t really address the issues we need to focus on growth”. He said the chancellor’s “real terms” freeze on business rates, for example, would lead to an additional £10m on M&S’s existing £170m rates bill.

Machin also criticised plans for the “most ridiculous” deposit return scheme under which retailers must install machines for the return of cans and plastic bottles in stores in England and Scotland from next October in order to drive up recycling rates. In Wales, the scheme is to also include glass bottles.

The M&S boss said he was “very supportive” of a new food trade deal between the UK and EU, which is expected to be completed later this month. Machin said this would help reduce a £10m post-Brexit hit to the retailer caused by the paperwork, packaging and logistical costs involved in sending goods to the Republic of Ireland and Northern Ireland. He added that the deal would also help UK exports of food to mainland Europe.

The comments emerged as M&S said a sustained cyber-attack that forced it to shut down its website last year had continued to hurt clothing sales over the past summer.

The retailer said profits bounced back to a better-than-expected £355m in the six months to 26 September from only £3.4m a year before, as it partly recovered from the hack. The attack, which began over Easter 2025, forced M&S to shut its website, which sells clothing and homewares, for more than six weeks.

Total sales over the half year across the food to fashion group rose 16.2% to £9.2bn.

M&S said sales and profit from its clothing and homewares arm remained below that of two years ago as “availability was constrained due to the longer-than-expected impact of manual ordering processes following the incident, while profitability was diluted by an increased mix of online sales, which have higher fulfilment costs”.

The retailer has previously explained that its team of buyers had to calculate demand and make orders to suppliers without the help of their usual technology and detailed information last year.

Machin said: “We are broadening our appeal and the brand is in good health, but the results fell short of their potential and show there is a lot more to do.”

He added that the manual ordering meant that in some clothing categories M&S did not stock enough everyday products, such as denim and knitwear, over the summer. Profits were also hit by more people shopping online during the “long hot summer”.

Machin said the impact of the cyber-attack “lasted longer than we expected”, but added: “That’s now behind us.”

M&S said it had been able to take its biggest ever share of a declining clothing market despite the availability problems, which were now resolved but added: “Market conditions remain challenging.”

Machin said the UK clothing market was down by 11%, with £1bn in clothing sales lost across the industry in the last six months as the hot weather, low consumer confidence and a shift away from spending on nonessentials hit sales.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

He added that the industry was still “heavily discounting” and M&S was trying to avoid that trend by pricing its goods affordably.

M&S clothing, beauty and homewares sales rose 17.5% to almost £2bn in the half year compared with the same period last year, which was affected by the hack.

Performance is expected to improve in the second half as M&S said availability has now recovered “creating a better balance between core and seasonal stock”.

Its food business powered ahead, increasing sales by almost 14% to £5.2bn in the half year as it opened seven new stores and expanded or refurbished others.

The group’s Ocado joint venture also swung to a half-year profit of £25.6m from a loss of £3.1m a year before as sales rose 16% and it improved the efficiency of its deliveries

M&S said it expected to cut £120m in costs this year across technology, logistics and stores. Machin said the retailer would not be making redundancies but was likely to be cautious on hiring new workers as it tried to offset new costs, including £35m related to a new packaging levy.

#172. South Africans vote in local elections in test for ANC

Failing public services, corruption and high crime rates have led to growing frustration among South African voters. That could lead to further losses for the ANC.

South Africans began voting Wednesday in municipal elections that could lead to fresh losses for the African National Congress (ANC).

The party of Nelson Mandela, which has led the country since the end of apartheid, has seen its popularity fall over the past decade.

In the previous local elections in 2021, it won less than 50% of the vote for the first time, and polls suggest it could drop below 40% this time.

Wednesday's vote is taking place amid rising frustration over failing public services, including unreliable water and electricity supplies, high crime rates, unemployment, and affordable housing. Concerns about undocumented migration also featured prominently in some campaigns.

The elections — seen as a gauge of public mood before national polls in 2029 — will decide who leads South Africa's eight main cities as well as 44 district municipalities and 205 local councils.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A crucial battleground is the city of Johannesburg. The economic hub is currently governed by a fragile ANC-led coalition, but polls suggest the liberal Democratic Alliance (DA), South Africa's second-biggest party, could win the most votes there.

The DA has put forward former national leader Helen Zille as candidate for Johannesburg mayor. The DA is also expected to retain control in the city of Cape Town.

Other closely watched areas include Tshwane, home to the administrative capital Pretoria, and the port city of Durban, where former President Jacob Zuma's MK party is mounting a strong challenge.

Voters do not directly elect mayors but choose municipal councillors, who then choose the mayors and form administrations that will run cities.

Although the ANC still has a loyal supporter base due to its role in ending apartheid, a growing number of South Africans are fed up with persisting problems such as high unemployment, power cuts and water shortages.

"Municipal failures have contributed to declining public trust and confidence ​in democratic governance," said President Cyril Ramaphosa, who acknowledged failures within his party, but also voiced optimism: "This election is an opportunity to turn this around."

The ANC lost its 30-year parliamentary majority in national elections in 2024, forcing it to enter into a national coalition with the DA and several other parties.

A further decline in the ANC's popularity at a local level could mean several cities will be ruled by unstable coalition governments.

More than 29 million citizens are registered to vote. But in the last municipal elections in 2021 less than half of them cast ballots.

Polls close at 9 p.m. local time (1900 GMT) and results are expected on Sunday.

#170. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#168. Germany's celebrated children's author Janosch has died

Bear, Tiger and Tiger-Duck: The author and illustrator Janosch achieved world fame with his heartwarming children's figures. He died at the age of 95.

Janosch, the pen name of German author and illustrator Horst Eckert, won hearts around the world with his warm and simple sense of humor. He charmed children and adults alike with his iconic Tiger, Bear, Tiger-Duck and other animals.

Janosch created more than 150 books, which have been translated into over 30 languages.

His best-selling children's book, "Oh, wie schön ist Panama," translated into English as "The Trip to Panama," was so lucrative that Janosch was able to settle down on the Canary Island of Tenerife in 1980, where he lived together with his wife Ines. The writer and illustrator died peacefully at the age of 95, his publisher Bernhard Feil announced on Wednesday.

Janosch was born as Horst Eckert on March 11, 1931, in Hindenburg (now Zabrze, Poland) in what was then Upper Silesia. After World War II, his family had to flee to Germany.

In 1953, Janosch started studying at the Academy of Art in Munich, thinking that it would be easy to become an artist. He was however dismissed after just a few terms for "lack of talent."

But luckily for his later readers, Janosch did not give up. He continued to work as a freelance artist, soon discovering his second great gift, namely writing.

His first children's book about a horse named Valek was published in 1960.

Although Janosch became famous primarily for his children's books, he also wrote books for adults that focused on his own childhood.

Even though he wrote cheerful books for children, Janosch hardly had any positive memories of his own childhood, as his father was an alcoholic. He was beaten not only by him but also by his mother and grandmother. Raised in a strictly Catholic environment, he was continuously threatened to be punished by God.

Later on, Janosch tried to overcome the burden of his childhood memories by turning to alcohol as well.

He always expressed a great deal of understanding for children, trying to encourage them to stand up for their rights. As a result of his childhood experiences, Janosch did not want to have children himself, and his attitude towards the Catholic Church always remained divided. "As I am a sinner and a heretic, God will let me live a long life so that I can return to the Church," he told his biographer Angela Bajorek.

Janosch's animal figures are endearing but not always well-behaved. They love all kinds of nonsense. But even if they lie to each other or put their own interests first, the true winner at the end of stories is always friendship and solidarity.

Janosch achieved his major breakthrough with "The Trip to Panama," a story about two friends, a little tiger and a bear, who set out to find their paradise in Panama. After touring around in circles, they finally end up where they started out; they realize that their journey was the destination. Janosch received the German Youth Book Prize in 1979 for the work.

Janosch's protagonists have become familiar to generations of children all over the world. They come up over and over again in the various stories carefully illustrated with water colors.

There is the honest lion Hans from the toy chest who fails to catch a nutcracker with his lies. There is Schnuddel and a ridiculous bragger Günter the Box-Frog. And then there is a tiger that collects mushrooms while looking out for his devoted friend, a bear, who always stands up for him even when the tiger temporarily leaves him because of a little pig in one of the stories.

The "Tigerente," literally "Tiger-Duck," a wooden duck on wheels with yellow-brown stripes, appears on numerous pictures by the illustrator. At times, the animal has been tossed into a corner, at times it is hanging from the ceiling, or the Box-Frog pulls it across the drawing.

His iconic figures, especially the Tiger-Duck, have served as motifs on various items, from cups to stamps to jewelry, and even on airplanes. Film adaptations and TV series based on his stories were produced in German and English.

Guests who visited Janosch in Tenerife were impressed by his very modest lifestyle. He always said that hanging around in his hammock doing nothing was what he enjoyed most. And yet Janosch remained very active until the end of his life.

From 2013 to 2019, he wrote a column for the German weekly Die Zeit, taking on the role of Mr. Wondrak, who commented on world affairs. Shortly before his 90th birthday in 2021, the complete collection of his Mr. Wondrak columns were published as a book in German.

Like the protagonists in his stories, Janosch always tried to content himself with few things. As he wrote in one of his books: "He who needs almost nothing, has everything."

This article was originally written in German.

→ Baca artikel asli

#167. Police say explosive devices found in Belfast are linked to ‘rightwing terrorism’

Suspect arrested after police uncover 17 improvised explosive devices during operation in Shankill Road area

An arsenal of explosive devices discovered in Northern Ireland is believed to be linked to far-right terrorism, police have said.

The stash of 17 improvised explosive devices and other material was found during searches in the Shankill Road area of Belfast on 30 and 31 October.

“The investigation is linked to extreme rightwing terrorism. There is nothing further at this time,” the Police Service of Northern Ireland said on Wednesday.

It was the largest haul of weaponry linked to the far right ever seized in Northern Ireland, the Belfast Telegraph reported.

The explosive devices were found with two detonators and five stun guns during a search and security operation. One man was arrested.

Wesley Thomas Fletcher, 35, of Leopold Street, appeared at Belfast magistrates court on Monday to face two charges of possession of explosives under suspicious circumstances and one charge of possession of a prohibited weapon, a Taser.

When asked if he understood the charges, Fletcher replied “Yes”. No application for bail was made and he was remanded in custody with the next hearing scheduled for 30 November.

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

#162. Louise Haigh and deputy Conservative leader Alex Burghart clash over welfare spending at PMQS – UK politics live

The first secretary of state says the government will bring welfare spending down

If you are deputising at PMQs, it may be nice to get a good write-up from the sketchwriters, and satisfying to craft a line that will play well on the TV or radio later, but, frankly, one task matters above all others: don’t mess up. And, in that respect, Louise Haigh and Alex Burghart both performed well – in exchanges likely to be quickly forgotten.

For Haigh, this was her first time, but it did not particularly sound like this. She sounded confident and authoritative, without being brash and hubristic. She did not have anything very new to say about welfare, and Labour does not have a firm answer yet to the question as to when it will cut the benefits bill, but Haigh swerved this quite adequately with attacks on the Tories’ record and by making the Labour argument about now wanting welfare spending on failure. She said:

double quotation markWe have been repeatedly clear that we cannot afford to pay for failure and must invest in success – and that is how we will bring the welfare bill down.

We will make support more conditional on taking up opportunities, ensuring young people get help they need earlier, tackling the long-term drivers of welfare spending, including high housing costs, and we will do all this in a Labour way.

She would not rule out tax rises to pay for more welfare spending (see 2.16pm), but that was the sort of question opposition parties ask when they can’t think of a proper, specific one and so instead have to resort to simplistic politics-by-slogan.

Burghart has done PMQs before, and he was a bit more polished. His pre-scripted riffs about “two 10-year plans in 10 months”, and John Healey’s Today programme welfare gaffe last week, were elegantly done, but not quite withering or funny enough to be truly damaging. He didn’t fail, but he didn’t score.

The most interesting question came from the Lib Dem deputy leader Daisy Cooper, who is probably the first MP to get a ‘Trump might be a rapist’ allegation into Hansard. (See 12.29pm.) Rather ingeniously, she tried to suggest there might be some sort of UK government angle. But there probably isn’t. This is one for Congress.

If anyone had a bad PMQs, it was probably Lindsay Hoyle. It was not clear who he was referring to when he delivered his ‘don’t shout a woman down’ bollocking to some Tory MP. (See 12.20pm.) But it sounded a bit chauvinist. Haigh is the second most senior person in government; she seemed perfectly capable of coping with a bit of heckling; and if shouting down a minister is unacceptable, it should not matter what sex they are.

Andy Burnham has said that he hopes the sweeping losses for Donald Trump’s Republican party in the US elections will give the world an “opportunity” to focus on ending the Iran war and easing the cost of living pressures.

In an interview at the Commonwealth summit in Antigua, asked if he was worried the results might make Trump more unpredictable, Burnham replied:

double quotation markPresident Trump has accepted the results, and I feel the world has an opportunity now to unite around the issue that is affecting us all, and that’s the cost of living.

That has been the issue that I have put front and centre in my first three months in Downing Street, and we’ve made moves.

But I can’t get people’s cost of living pressures down until the conflict in the Middle East is resolved. The conflict in Ukraine started by Russia.

These are the things we have to focus on now, and I hope the world has a moment to do that. And certainly, in my calls with leaders, that is what I will be asking everybody to do.

Some politicians and commentators have interpreted the results as evidence that rightwing populism is in retreat around the world. But when asked if he thought the elections were part of a global pattern, Burnham was reluctant to make that claim. Instead, he replied:

double quotation markI think people want to see the world coming together and focusing on the things that matter to people, and that’s what the voters, in my view, are saying in the United States.

Let’s get focused in a laser-like way on those cost of living issues. And I think around the world there’s a recognition that countries can’t do that on their own, and can’t do that when we have division between nations.

I’ve been working hard as prime minister to bring a new approach, a new approach in the Middle East, and the foreign secretary set that out [with the announcements about sanctions on illegal Israeli settlement in the West Bank].

A new approach to Europe, and we believe that will help us get growth back up and reduce those cost of living pressures. But a new approach with the Commonwealth too.

Asked if Britain would be pushing for de-escalation in the Iran war, Burnham said:

double quotation markI’ll be working for de-escalation. To make it very clear, we want to see a resolution, the Strait of Hormuz fully opened again. That’s what we will be working towards.

Helen Maguire (Lib Dem) asks about conditions at Epsom hospital, where urgent repairs are needed.

Haigh says she is aware of the problems. The minister is happy to meet Maguire to discuss the situation, she says.

Munira Wilson (Lib Dem) says Haigh recently said the government would give local leaders more power to invest in things that matter. But it is cutting funding for Richmond council by more than £100m over the next four years, 60% of its budget? Will Haigh meet her to discuss this to find a solution.

Haigh says the government inherited an unfair council funding system. She offers Wilson a meeting with a minister.

Bobby Dean (Lib Dem) asks if the government will back legislation to make US tech companies pay fair compensation to victims of online scams. He says, given the US election results, the government should be tougher with the US administration.

Haigh says the Online Safety Act requires tech companies to take robust action on online scams.

Andrew Lewin (Lab) asks about support for Hatfield.

Haigh says the government is investing funding in local growth.

Bradley Thomas (Con) asks if Haigh agrees with a minister who said people how start their own businesses are not working people.

This is a reference to an answer Emma Reynolds, chief secretary to the Treasury, gave in an interview last week.

Haigh says Thomas is referring to the sort of people the government is trying to help.

Daisy Cooper, the Lib Dem deputy leader, says US voters have rejected the policies of Donald Trump. But Trump is threatening more strikes against Iran. Will the government tell him “no more strikes”.

Haigh says the government’s policy is de-escalation. She says she hopes the Tories and Reform UK will reconsider their decision to back the war.

Cooper says US voters are also sick of Trump’s scandals, including an allegation of rape. Will Haigh join her in saying we will not tolerate powerful men who abuse women? And will the UK support any investigations that Congress launches.

double quotation markThe American people have also sent a message that they are sick of Trump’s scandals, from his blatant corruption to his close friendship with Epstein and, most recently emerging from the Epstein files, an allegation of rape. Will the first secretary of state join me in saying that in this house we will not tolerate powerful men who abuse their position against women in this parliament or any other? And will the government commit to assisting any and all inquiries that the new Democratic Congress may launch?

double quotation markThe honourable lady raises an incredibly important point about the Epstein files and everything that they revealed. She’s absolutely right to highlight the importance, always of challenging power and standing on behalf of vulnerable victims.

Cooper is referring to allegations contained in some of the Epstein files, recently set out at some length in a report from the Democrats on the House judiciary committee.

This is probably the first time rape allegations about Trump have been raised in the Commons.

Burghart says the Labour way is more welfare, more tax and more borrowing. They are too weak to do the right thing, he claims. Everyone else is paying the price.

double quotation markIf Labour doesn’t want to do the right thing, why doesn’t it just get out of the way?

The Speaker reprimands a Tory MP for “trying to shout a woman down”.

Haigh says Burghart, a historian, once wrote a paper titled “Profile of a Doomed Elite”. It could have been about the shadow cabinet, she suggests.

She stresses the government’s achievements.

double quotation markThis administration has in our first 100 days [been] cutting business rates for 70,000 businesses, cutting tax for entrepreneurs, cutting VAT from electricity bills, helping 20 million households with energy bills, scrapping standing charges, capping bus fares, investing in our high streets, extending parental pay, tackling rough sleeping.

Burghart says the government won’t fix welfare. The Milburn review was delayed until after the budget. And Pat McFadden once said Labour MPs just wanted more welfare spending. So will the government commit to not putting up taxes to pay for more benefits.

Haigh says the government will bring welfare spending down, but ensuring that it now longer pays for failure. She says Burghart is on record as saying the Tories should apologise for what happened when they were in office. But Kemi Badenoch was in cabinet at the time.

Burghart says, in her comments about the budget, Haigh missed out the bits “where the Resolution Foundation said that this budget would be catastrophic for living standards” and where the OBR said tax rises could cost jobs.

double quotation markI’m old enough to remember Keir Starmer’s 10-year plan because it was six months ago. This is the only political party in history to have two 10-year plans in ten months.

Haigh says Burghart can ask the same question, but he will get the same answer.

Burghart says last week John Healey said in an interview he would bring welfare spending down in this parliament. This caused panic in Downing Street, he says. He goes on:

double quotation markThe prime minister was sent out to clarify things. He said the welfare bill needs to come down, but this can’t be done by cutting the welfare bill.

Then, then at teatime, the chancellor conceded defeat and said it’s not correct to say that we’re going to bring the welfare bill down.

So will the first secretary of state be clear? Why isn’t Labour going to bring the welfare down.

Haigh says Burghart was not listening to here earlier answer. And she criticises the Tories for voting against the budget last night.

double quotation markThey voted against a council tax cut for 20 million households. They voted against VAT of electricity bills. They voted against taking 70,000 small businesses out of business rates, and they voted against a tax on banks to fund it all.

Burghart says the figure is £183bn. He goes on:

double quotation markThat’s the highest figure ever. It’s higher than anything during Covid. It is higher than defence schools and policing combined. It is nearly £10,000 for every working household in this country. So will the government commit to bring that bill down in this parliament?

Haigh says the government is taking measures to bring welfare spending down.

Alex Burghart starts with a short question. How much will the government spend on working age welfare?

Haigh congratulates Burghart on his job. He has said he will correct Kemi Badenoch when she is wrong. That will make him the busiest man in Westminster, she says.

She says the government is committed to getting welfare spending down. Under the Tories, welfare spending rose by £88bn, she says.

Matthew Patrick (Lab) asks Haigh to restore the opportunities for young people that Brexit took away.

Haigh says she agrees. She says an SPS deal with the EU will help the economy, and the government is also negotiating a youth experience scheme. She says Alex Burghart has already promised to rip up these deals.

#159. BMW reports lower Q3 earnings as China woes mount

BMW reports a drop in profits in the July to September quarter amid fierce competition from China.

German carmaker BMW reported a nearly 15% drop in third-quarter net profit on Wednesday, as competition from China shakes the global car industry.

Net profit for the third quarter fell to 1.45 billion euros ($1.61 billion), while sales fell nearly 6% to 30.45 billion euros, BMW said.

The main cause of the decline was BMW's poor business in China. Although the company sold more cars in the US and Europe compared to the same period last year, this could only partially offset the decline in China.

BMW's car deliveries in China, which were already at their lowest level since 2017 last year, fell 24.8% in the quarter and consequently dragged global deliveries down by 6.3.

CEO Milan Nedeljkovic said his company would "specifically intensify and accelerate structural measures."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

BMW on Tuesday also lowered its outlook for the current year, citing the faster-than-planned implementation of restructuring measures that includes 8,000 layoffs in its home market.

German carmakers have been struggling to compete with their Chinese counterparts in the electric vehicle market,  resulting in cost-cutting measures.

Volkswagen, the world's second-largest carmaker, is battling mounting competition from falling demand in its core Chinese market.

BMW, and its industry heavyweight peers, Volkswagen and Mercedes-Benz, have announced plans to scale back production and reduce costs.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Volkswagen Group posted its worst quarterly results in more than a decade earlier this October.

It is looking to cut about 15% of its workforce worldwide, or 100,000 jobs, by the end of the decade. BMW has announced that it will cut up to 8,000 jobs — about 5% of its workforce — by the end of 2027.

On the other hand, German auto parts supplier Schaeffler announced on Tuesday that it planned to cut almost 3,500 jobs and close five sites in Europe, by 2028.

According to the company statement, three-fourths of the 3,460 job cuts and four of the five site closures will be in Germany.

German automotive supplier Bosch, the world's biggest, announced last year that it would cut 13,000 jobs, mostly in its auto division.

#158. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

What’s the difference between Persians and Arabs?Comedian @MazJobrani shares with me his thoughts on touring the Middle East, his support for Iran’s protests, and his latest comedy special: pic.twitter.com/UvYwVgJIlw

China and Brazil have reached an agreement to ditch USD and use their own currencies—China Daily pic.twitter.com/q33U4kVO5Q

An Iranian state news outlet is gloating at what it sees as the demise of the US dollar.

The meme was pinned to the top of IRNA’s Twitter page, and was met with laughter and ridicule. “Dream on,” said another user, pointing to the dollar’s use as the main reserve currency around the world.

China Daily said that the agreement was part of “the rising global use of the Chinese renminbi.” It would reportedly enable China and Brazil to conduct trade and financial transactions using local currencies instead of the dollar.

#155. Myanmar’s Aung San Suu Kyi ‘sounds strong’ but is isolated, son says

Kim Aris tells the Guardian chance to speak with detained Nobel laureate was ‘public relations’ move from military leaders

Myanmar’s former democratic leader Aung San Suu Kyi appears to be in good physical and mental health but is living in near-complete isolation, her son has said after the first call with his 81-year-old mother in more than five years.

“She still sounds certainly all there mentally, and physically she sounds strong,” Kim Aris said. “I don’t think she has anybody outside of the military or the police around her, so essentially she’s been held in solitary confinement from what I gather.”

After being deposed in a 2021 military coup, the Nobel Peace prize laureate has been moved to an unspecified location in the capital. “I’m still not entirely sure where she is,” said Aris. “I think it’s a residence in Naypyidaw, which is fenced off and it has a scrubby bit of land around it.”

Aris, who has lived in the UK most of his life and whose late father was a British historian, was allowed to speak to his mother on Thursday at the Myanmar embassy in London. While he welcomed the chance to talk, he said the invitation was part of a “public relations” campaign by the country’s military leaders.

The former junta chief Min Aung Hlaing has sought to rebuild international diplomatic ties, and in April switched his military fatigues for civilian clothes after elections that were widely condemned as a sham.

Activists have long demanded “proof of life” for Aung San Suu Kyi, as well as scores of other political prisoners. In August, she was finally photographed during a Red Cross visit. Last week, a government spokesperson published images of her smiling in a wood-panelled room as officials delivered two gardening books given to her as gifts by Britain’s King Charles. A paper calendar on the wall behind her showed the date.

Aris said these photo-ops were well-known stunts that the regime had used before to pretend his mother, a national icon, was being treated fairly. The daughter of the assassinated national independence hero Gen Aung San has spent much of her life since the late 1980s in and out of house arrest for leading pro-democracy movements.

“She remains completely shut off from the rest of the world. I think she’s unaware of what’s happening outside of where she is because she’s not allowed any newspapers or radio or television,” Aris said. “She’s been locked up for five years on trumped-up, politically motivated charges and she should be free.”

Ruled for decades by generals and suffering from western sanctions, Myanmar experienced a brief democratic moment after Aung San Suu Kyi was allowed to run in a 2015 election that her party overwhelmingly won. She took up the role of “state counsellor” under a civilian-military power-sharing agreement.

Her international reputation was soon tarnished after the 2016-2017 mass killings and expulsions of Rohingya Muslims. Aung San Suu Kyi flew to the international court of justice in The Hague, where she said she acknowledged military units might have used “disproportionate force”, but defended the government against accusations of genocide.

Aris said that his mother was never “silent” on the Rohingya crisis and what she said on the issue “could fill volumes”. Under her leadership, Myanmar launched a domestic inquiry and appointed the former UN chief Kofi Annan to head a commission.

He added that as state counsellor, Aung San Suu Kyi was “always walking on a tightrope”, knowing the military could strip her of her fragile power at any moment.

“The very hard-won nascent democracy that she was trying to lead was holding on to things by a thread,” he said. “The military could have taken over at any point, and they were doing their best to make my mother’s situation a lot more difficult and to undermine her position. And I think in that respect they did a pretty good job.”

The 2021 power grab by the military triggered a civil war that has killed more than 100,000 people, according to conflict monitors.

Aris said the level of public global support for his mother had “dwindled incredibly” since the Rohingya crisis and that the whole country was now suffering from a lack of international attention. While many governments have called for Aung San Suu Kyi’s release, she is not treated as the beloved international figure that she once was.

“Never mind my mother, the fact that people ignore what’s happening in Burma shouldn’t be down to what was being done to the Rohingya,” he said, using the country’s former name. “And that seems to be the case sometimes, that people say: ‘Oh, well, look how the Rohingya were treated. We can ignore what’s happening in Burma.’”

#153. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.
